I'll be standing there looking at a project with nothing but a tape measure in my hands when I knew darn well I needed my socket set. It’s embarrassing, honestly, having to walk all the way back home to my garage because my brain just didn't hold onto the thought of what I was doing. It’s just... it's happening all the time now, and it's driving me up a wall.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these memory problems, and have you noticed any other changes in your thinking, mood, or ability to carry out daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about the exact timing on that, because it hasn't been my whole life, I can tell you that much. It really started getting under my skin and causing real, actual trouble about... well, it would be a year and two months ago now. Yeah, just about fourteen months. I remember the timing exactly because it was right after I had this cardiac scare. Gave my daughter a real fright, let me tell you, and the doctors were all over me about taking it easy and resting. \n\nSee, before that happened, I was always on the move. Always. I'd be walking miles around the neighborhood every single day, taking on every little heavy-duty project I could get my hands on. Tearing down old sheds, building things, hauling materials—just constantly busy. And doing all that, keeping my hands and my feet moving, it... it kept me settled, if that makes any sense? Like my engine was burning the right amount of fuel, so my mind was clear and I knew what I was doing. \n\nBut then the heart thing happened, and they told me I had to cut way back on the walking and the big projects. Forced me to sit in my house a lot more than I ever wanted to. And ever since I had to slow down physically, it’s like... well, that's when all this forgetting really started causing havoc. As for other changes in my mood or thinking, I don't know about all that, except that I just feel completely unsettled all the time now because I can't just go, go, go like I used to. It's like since I was forced to stop moving so much, my brain just stopped keeping track of what I'm supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, down? Hopeless? No, I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, sure, I get frustrated, like I was saying, because I can't just jump up and tear down a shed or walk five miles like I used to, but I don't feel sad or hopeless about life in general. I'm just... restless, I guess. \n\nAnd as for losing interest, no, not a bit! I still want to do all the things I've always loved doing. I still want to be out there fixing things, tinkering in the garage, helping the neighbors with their gutters or whatever they need. I still *try* to do the smaller jobs, like fixing those hinges I mentioned. The interest is absolutely still there, 100 percent. The problem isn't that I don't *want* to do things, it's that I get out there and realize I forgot my tools, or I start a project and get sidetracked because I can't seem to hold onto the plan in my head. \n\nSo no, I'm not depressed or anything like that. I'm not sitting around crying or feeling sorry for myself, I'm just incredibly annoyed that my brain isn't cooperating with what I still very much want to be doing. If my body and my memory would just let me, I'd be out there working from sunup to sundown just like I always did. It's just... it's the restriction that's driving me crazy, not a lack of wanting to do it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself confused about where you are, or gotten lost in places you’re very familiar with, like your own neighborhood?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I mean, I know exactly where I am. I’ve lived in this neighborhood for, gosh, it’s gotta be thirty-five years now. My wife and I bought the house back in '88, right before our daughter started kindergarten, and I know every single crack in the sidewalk on our street. I know that the Miller's oak tree drops acorns all over the corner of Elm and Maple, and I know that you have to jiggle the handle on the Johnsons' side gate if you want it to latch right. I could walk these streets blindfolded, honestly. \n\nAnd even now, with all the forgetting and the... the frustration of not having my tools when I show up to fix that gate for the Johnsons, I never actually get lost on the way there. I know exactly where I’m going. I just... I get there and realize I don't have the wrench. Or I'll be walking down to the hardware store—well, the one that used to be on Main Street before they moved it out to the highway, I still catch myself walking that direction sometimes out of habit, but I know it's not there anymore. I'm not confused about it, it's just muscle memory, you know? \n\nBut as far as actually being lost or not knowing where I am? Never. My internal compass is just fine. It's just the details of what I'm supposed to be doing when I get to where I'm going that seem to slip right through the cracks. It’s like my brain is a sieve for the important stuff, but I can still tell you exactly how to get to the old Miller place, no problem. So, no, getting lost isn't an issue at all. Just the... the other stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep? Like trouble falling asleep, staying asleep, or maybe sleeping more than usual?\n\nPatient: Sleep? No, no changes there. I’ve always been a pretty solid sleeper, to be honest with you. My wife, now, she’ll toss and turn and complain about the mattress—we got one of those foam ones about five years ago, maybe six, and she says it gets too hot—but me? I hit the pillow and I am out cold. \n\nI don't have any trouble falling asleep at all. Never have. Even with all this restless energy I was telling you about, all that pacing around, by the time nighttime rolls around, I'm just done. I'll sit down in my recliner, watch a little TV, maybe the evening news or one of those car restoration shows I like, and then I go to bed around 10:30, just like clockwork. And staying asleep? Not a problem either. I mean, sure, I might get up once in the middle of the night to use the restroom, but at my age, well, you know how that goes, that's just standard plumbing maintenance, right? But I go right back to bed and fall right back to sleep. \n\nAnd I definitely don't sleep more than usual. Gosh, I couldn't sleep in if you paid me a million bucks. My eyes pop open at 6:00 AM every single morning, just like they did when I was working at the plant for forty years. The sun comes up, and I'm up. I'm awake and I'm ready to go. Which, honestly, just brings me right back to the whole problem I was talking about earlier—I wake up with all this drive to get out there and *do* something, to get out in the garage or fix something around the yard, and then I just... I end up pacing around the kitchen with my coffee because the doctors say I'm not supposed to be doing the heavy lifting anymore. And then I get sidetracked and forget what I was even planning to do with my morning in the first place! \n\nBut the sleep itself? No, the sleep is perfectly fine. Exact same as it's always been, no trouble there whatsoever.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how you feel emotionally—like feeling indifferent, less responsive to things that used to matter to you, or just not as engaged with life?\n\nPatient: No, no, definitely not indifferent. I mean, if anything, I care too much! I’m still just as engaged with everything as I ever was. I mean, take my grandkids, for instance. My daughter, Sarah—the one who started kindergarten right after we bought the house, remember?—she’s got two boys now, seven and nine, absolute terrors but I love ‘em to pieces. I’m always out there trying to build them things. I tried to build them a treehouse last summer. Well, I say \"tried,\" I got the plans all drawn up, bought the lumber, hauled it all into the backyard... and then, well, you know how it goes with me lately. I started on the ladder, got distracted because the saw blade was dull, went to the hardware store to get a new blade, ended up talking to Gary down at the checkout for an hour, came back, and completely forgot I was even working on the treehouse until Sarah asked about it three days later. The lumber's still sitting under a tarp out back. \n\nBut the point is, I *wanted* to build it for them. I care about it. I’m not sitting around staring at the wall, not caring about my family or my hobbies. I still read the newspaper every single morning, cover to cover, well, mostly. I start at the front page, but then an article on the local zoning board will remind me about a meeting I meant to go to, and then I’m up and pacing around the kitchen trying to find the flyer about the meeting, and then I forget what I was looking for. But I’m engaged! I care about the zoning laws! I care about the neighborhood, I care about my family, I care about my projects. \n\nI’m just as responsive as I always was. If my wife says, \"Frank, the sink is leaking,\" I don't just shrug and ignore it. I jump right up! I'm ready to fix it! I just... I might go to the garage for a wrench and end up reorganizing the whole toolbox because I saw a loose screw, and then two hours later my wife is asking why the sink is still leaking and I haven't even touched it. But emotionally? Indifferent? Not at all. I feel everything just as strongly as I always did. It’s just the follow-through that’s completely shot to pieces. I'm engaged, I'm just... all over the place with it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble planning or organizing things, like following a recipe, managing your finances, or keeping track of the steps in a project you're working on?\n\nPatient: Trouble planning? No, no, the planning is actually the easy part for me! I don't have any trouble organizing things or figuring out the steps. I mean, take the finances, for example. I’ve been balancing our checkbook every single Sunday morning since, well, since we got married. I sit down at the kitchen table with my coffee, I get out my calculator—it’s this old Casio one with the big buttons, none of this smartphone nonsense where the screen turns off right when you're looking at a number—and I go through every single receipt. I organize everything into these color-coded folders my wife bought at the office supply store years ago. The electric bill goes in the blue folder, the water bill in the green one, property taxes in the red one... I know exactly where every penny goes, down to the cent. Never miss a payment, never get confused about the math. \n\nAnd as for projects? Gosh, I can plan a project out perfectly. Like I was saying about that treehouse earlier, I didn't just wing it. I had blueprints drawn up to scale! I spent three evenings sitting in my recliner figuring out exactly how many two-by-fours I needed, exactly what gauge of screws to buy, the whole sequence of what to build first so the platform would be level... I can keep track of all the steps in my head and on paper just fine. I write these beautiful, detailed lists for everything. I've got yellow legal pads full of them out in the garage right now. \n\nSo, no, it's not the planning that's the issue at all. I know exactly what needs to be done and in what order. I can organize a project or a budget with my eyes closed. It’s just... well, like I said before, I'll have this perfect list, and I'll go to grab the very first tool I need to start step one, and then I see a squeaky hinge on the cabinet, and suddenly I'm oiling all the doors in the house and the list is just sitting on the workbench forgotten. But the actual organizing of it? The figuring out how it's supposed to work? No, I'm sharp as a tack with that. Never had an issue with the planning side of things, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your memory and ability to keep track of things is worse now compared to how it was a few years ago, before your heart scare?\n\nPatient: Worse now? Well, yeah, I suppose it is. I mean, it’s not like my actual brain just suddenly broke, you know? But as far as it causing me real trouble—the getting sidetracked and completely losing track of what I'm doing—yeah, it's definitely worse. I'd say it’s been a real, noticeable problem for about a year and two months now. \n\nBecause it all goes right back to that cardiac scare you mentioned. Let me tell you, before that happened, I was always, always on the move. I'd be out walking the neighborhood for miles every single day, rain or shine, or I'd be out in the yard hauling bags of topsoil, building fences, really doing the heavy lifting for all my projects. And doing all that... well, it kept me settled. It gave me a place to put all this restless energy I have. I could burn it off, and then my head would be clear. \n\nBut then the scare happened—which, by the way, puts the absolute fear of God in you, waking up in that hospital bed with all those machines beeping and my poor wife looking like she hadn't slept in a week—and the doctors came in shaking their heads, telling me I had to take it easy. They forced me to cut back on the walking, and they said absolutely no more heavy, strenuous projects. So for the last year and two months, I haven't had my usual outlets. And without all that heavy work and walking to keep me settled, I'm just buzzing around the house like a trapped fly. I've got all this drive and nowhere to put it, so I just start a dozen little things and can't keep track of a darn one of them. So, yes, it's definitely been worse since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in your motivation or drive to start or finish tasks, even if you know they’re important?\n\nPatient: Decrease? No, no, doc, that’s not it at all. If anything, my motor is running too hot! I don't have any lack of drive. I wake up every single morning just raring to go. I’ve got a whole list of things in my head that I want to tackle, and I'm practically itching to get started on them. \n\nJust to give you an example, take last Tuesday. I woke up at 5:30 in the morning—couldn't sleep, you know how it is, the sun was just starting to peek over the horizon. I looked out the front window and saw the gutters were absolutely overflowing with leaves from that big oak tree we have in the front yard. Beautiful tree, planted it myself back in '85—well, maybe '86, right after that awful blizzard we had—but it sheds like a golden retriever in the fall. Anyway, I was incredibly motivated! I knew it was an important job. If you don't clean those gutters, the water backs up, gets under the shingles, rots the fascia boards, and then you're looking at thousands of dollars in roof repairs. My neighbor Tom had to deal with that last year, had scaffolding up for weeks, absolute nightmare. \n\nSo I didn't even wait to have my morning coffee. I put my work boots on, marched straight out to the shed to get the extension ladder. The drive to start was a hundred percent there! I wanted to do it. I was excited to get it crossed off the list. It’s just... well, like I was telling you before, I got out to the shed, and when I pulled the door open, the hinges gave this awful, loud squeak. So I thought, \"I'll just grab the WD-40 real quick so it doesn't wake my wife.\" I go into the garage to find the oil, and while I'm digging on the shelf, I knock over a box of old spark plugs I meant to take to the recycling center. So I start picking those up, which reminds me I need to check the spark plug on the lawnmower. \n\nThree hours later, my wife comes out to the garage, and I've got the lawnmower completely dismantled on the floor, I'm covered in grease, and the ladder is still sitting untouched by the shed. But I wasn't unmotivated! I didn't sit down on a bucket and think, \"Oh, I just don't have the energy for the gutters.\" I had plenty of energy! I was working up a sweat! I just ended up using all that drive on the lawnmower, and then on reorganizing the workbench, and then on sweeping the garage floor. I'm going a hundred miles an hour all day long, from sunup to sundown. So, no, motivation isn't the problem at all. The gas pedal works perfectly fine—it's just the steering wheel that's got a mind of its own!\n\nDoctor: Would you say there are things in your life now that you just don’t care about anymore, even if you used to care a lot?\n\nPatient: Don't care? Oh, heavens, no. No, I haven't lost interest in a single thing. If anything, doc, I care too much! My wife always tells me I get too invested in every little thing, and she's probably right. I mean, I still have exactly the same passions I had thirty, forty years ago. \n\nTake my vegetable garden, for instance. I've been growing heirloom tomatoes out back since... well, we bought the house in '78, but I didn't put the raised beds in until the spring of '81. Or maybe '82? No, '81, because that was the year my brother helped me haul the railroad ties in his old Ford pickup, the blue one with the rusted out tailgate. Anyway, I still care about those tomatoes just as much today as I did back then. I'm out there every morning checking the soil moisture, looking for hornworms under the leaves, pinching the suckers off the main stems... I mean, you have to stay on top of it, or they just grow into a wild, tangled jungle and you don't get any decent fruit. I care deeply about getting a good harvest. \n\nAnd my woodworking, too! I still absolutely love getting out in the shop. I care about getting the dovetail joints perfectly flush, picking out the right stain—usually I go with a dark walnut, though I've been experimenting with a lighter cherry finish lately on some birdhouses I'm making for the grandkids. I still read all my woodworking magazines cover to cover the day they arrive in the mail, I still watch the restoration shows on the television... \n\nSo, no, I haven't lost my passion for anything. I still care deeply about all my hobbies, keeping the house in good shape, my family... I haven't stopped caring about a darn thing. I just, you know, have trouble staying on one of those things at a time, like I was saying before with the lawnmower and the gutters. But the actual caring? The interest? That hasn't faded one bit. I'm as interested in life as I ever was!\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in your ability to start tasks, even when you know they’re important and want to do them?\n\nPatient: Start tasks? No, doc, I feel like I'm repeating myself a bit here, but no, I don't have any trouble starting things. Starting is the easiest part! Like I said about the gutters, I jump right in. If anything, I start too many things! \n\nJust... well, just look at this past weekend. My wife, she asked me to bring the heavier winter coats up from the cedar closet in the basement. Very important task, the mornings are getting real crisp, you know, there was actual frost on my windshield by 6 AM on Saturday. So I didn't put it off. I didn't sit in my recliner and stare at the television. I headed straight down those basement stairs the very second she asked. The ability to start is a hundred percent intact! \n\nBut, see, the cedar closet is all the way in the back corner, past the utility sink. And as I'm walking past the sink, I notice the faucet is dripping. Just a little drip, drip, drip. Now, I know that's just a worn O-ring, probably hasn't been changed since we put that sink in back in... oh, must have been '98, when we finished that half of the basement so the kids would have a place to play pool. So I immediately start fixing the sink. I go grab my wrench, I turn off the water main, I'm taking the handles apart. I started that task without a second thought! And then while I'm looking for the replacement O-ring in my plumbing box, I see a pipe snake and remember the upstairs guest tub is draining slow, so I grab that and head upstairs. \n\nSo by noon, I've started the coat retrieval, I've started the sink repair, and I've started unclogging the tub. I'm a starting machine! I can start things all day long. It's just... well, my wife had to go down and get the coats herself eventually, and we didn't have water to the house for three hours because I forgot I shut the main off. But the actual ability to get up and begin a job? No decrease there whatsoever. I'm always ready to dive right in.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you have trouble staying focused on a task once you start it, or do you find yourself getting distracted very easily?\n\nPatient: Oh, bingo. That’s the nail right on the head, doc. Staying focused? It’s like trying to keep a butterfly on a leash! Getting distracted is... well, that's the whole crux of the issue, really. I get distracted by absolutely every little thing going on around me. \n\nI mean, just take my Thursdays down at the senior center. I go there most weeks—usually just Thursdays because that's when they make the real brewed coffee, not that awful instant powder they put out on Tuesdays, which just tastes like brown water to me. Anyway, I'll be sitting there at one of those plastic folding tables. Just last week, I was supposed to be looking over the maintenance and repair list for the community board, because they all know I'm handy with tools and such. And at the same time, a few of the guys and I had a game of Pinochle going. So I’m sitting there, I’ve got the list right in front of me, I’ve got my cards in my hand, and I have every intention of focusing on the task.\n\nBut then... well, somebody across the room coughs. Just a regular old cough! And my head snaps right over. I'm suddenly wondering if Frank is coming down with a cold, or if I should go dig in my coat pocket to find him a lozenge. And before I can even look back at my hand of cards, somebody pushes back from a table and a chair scrapes on that cheap linoleum floor. You know that awful, loud squealing sound? So, of course, my attention shoots over there to see who's getting up. And then, while my head is turned, I happen to see someone walking past the senior-center window outside—just a fella walking a little terrier mix, looked like—and suddenly I'm watching him, wondering if he lives in those new condos they just built down the block. \n\nAnd I'm just completely off with that! I'm watching the dog, listening to the chairs, looking at Frank, and I've completely abandoned the repair list and the card game right in front of me. The guys are tapping the table going, 'Hey, it's your turn, play a card!' and I've totally lost track of what trump is, and I haven't read a single word on the maintenance sheet. So, yes, if there's a noise, a movement, anything at all, my brain just drops whatever I'm doing and chases right after it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts feel slower than they used to, or that it takes you longer to get started on things even when you know you should?\n\nPatient: Slower? Oh, heavens no. No, no, no. If anything, doc, it’s the exact opposite! My thoughts are going a mile a minute. I mean, I wish they would slow down just a fraction, to be honest with you. It’s like a pinball machine up there most days! Just bouncing from one thing to the next. \n\nLike, I’ll be sitting on the back patio with my wife—we got this nice little wrought-iron patio set at a yard sale over in the next town a few years back, sanded it all down by hand and painted it a nice forest green so it would match the trim on the garage—anyway, we’ll be sitting there trying to enjoy a quiet glass of iced tea, and my brain is just buzzing. I'm thinking about the tomatoes, and then I'm thinking about if I need to change the spark plug in the lawnmower, and then I'm wondering what the grandkids are doing for their spring vacation, and then I'm trying to remember if I turned the hose off... it never stops. So no, my thoughts haven't slowed down one bit. I'm as sharp as I ever was, just maybe a little too fast for my own good sometimes! \n\nAnd as for getting started... well, like I was saying just a minute ago with the cedar closet and the winter coats, I really don't have any trouble getting going. I know I tend to talk your ear off and maybe we get a little sidetracked, but just to be absolutely clear on that point, I am out of the gate like a racehorse the second something needs doing. I don't sit around waiting to start. I don't procrastinate. I jump right in! Sometimes I jump in before I even know how deep the water is, so to speak. Like when I decided to fix the toaster last month instead of just buying a new one, took the whole thing apart on the kitchen table before my wife even finished her toast... \n\nSo, no, no sluggishness here, doc. Not in my head and not in my feet. I'm always moving, always thinking!\n\nDoctor: Would you say you often feel physically restless or have a hard time sitting still for very long?\n\nPatient: Oh, boy, do I ever. Sitting still? It’s... well, it’s practically torture for me, doc. It really is. My wife is always telling me to just sit down, relax, watch a program with her for once. And I try, I really do! I'll get into my recliner—it's one of those nice leather ones with the built-in lumbar support, we got a great deal on it over at the furniture liquidators about five years ago—and I'll try to just watch the evening news or Jeopardy or whatever she's got on the television. \n\nBut I just... I can't do it for more than a few minutes. I'm tapping my foot, I'm drumming my fingers on the armrests, I'm adjusting the tilt on the chair back. Because, see, lately I've had to sort of... well, just pace myself a bit. Just the usual getting-older stuff, you know, the knees aren't what they were, so I can't be up on the ladder all afternoon or walking five miles a day like I used to. But since I’ve had to slow down on my walks and projects, sitting still makes me feel like I’ve got a motor running inside me and no good place to put it! \n\nIt's just humming away in there, revving up, and I have absolutely nowhere to direct all that energy. So I end up getting up to check the thermostat, or I go to the kitchen to see if the refrigerator door is shut tight—which, by the way, the magnetic seal on that thing is definitely starting to go, I really need to look up the model number and order a new gasket for it—and before I know it, I'm pacing back and forth across the living room rug while they're doing the Final Jeopardy category. I just can't stay planted in one spot. That motor just keeps running and running, and if I don't get up and move around, I feel like I'm going to jump right out of my own skin!"
